# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-11-04

| **[WebHarbor: Docking Real Websites for Evolving GUI Agent Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03634v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>GUI web agents require realistic and interactive environments that remain stable during both evaluation and training. Live website evaluation suffers from changing content, access restrictions, and limited coverage of authenticated workflows, while existing synthetic environments for training often lack functional depth and visual fidelity. We propose WebHarbor, a suite of 153 executable and dockerized website environments that faithfully reconstruct the visual interfaces, content, and functional workflows of real-world web services under deterministic control. We build WebHarbor through task-driven human-agent collaboration, where environments evolve as new tasks expand their functional scope, coding agents implement the required changes, and human contributors audit visual fidelity, functional correctness, and task validity. The resulting suite comprises 3,518 tasks spanning multi-step and post-authentication workflows, where existing works often fail to cover. Beyond reliable evaluation, WebHarbor provides high-fidelity synthetic web environments for cost-efficient reinforcement learning (RL). We demonstrate that a Qwen 4B-level GUI agent trained with RL in WebHarbor generalizes to unseen tasks and performs competitively with leading open-source GUI web agents across four benchmarks. The code and data are publicly available at https://github.com/aiming-lab/WebHarbor.</p></details> | 31 pages |
| **[Drafting Agents, Not Just Tokens: Speculative Recursive Self-Improvement for Agentic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16491v2)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Improving an agent through successive revisions requires balancing exploration and evaluation under a limited budget. A narrow search can miss useful sequences of edits, while fully evaluating every intermediate version often exhausts the budget before those sequences yield a verified improvement. Inspired by the draft--verify principle of speculative decoding, we propose Speculative Recursive Self-Improvement (SpecRSI), a two-level framework coupling inner-loop agent search with outer-loop improver learning. SpecRSI drafts successors before their predecessors are fully evaluated, while adopting only fully evaluated, admissible versions. In the inner loop, Verification-Aware Tree Search (VATS) allocates the budget between further edits and outstanding checks using the predicted verified return of a continuation. In the outer loop, Group Verification Optimization (GVO) updates the improver from completed searches while keeping the real evaluator fixed. We evaluate SpecRSI on scientific optimization (including active queue management (AQM) for network infrastructure), coding, and general agent-system benchmarks. Under matched evaluation-call budgets, SpecRSI improves scientific terminal gain over Janus by 60.0%--96.3% and area under the budget curve by 32.1%--91.6%, while achieving higher means across all 50 scientific backbone--task pairs and outperforming MetaRSI-v1 by 1.30--2.70 percentage points (pp) in system score across twenty backbones. On instance-level comparisons, it further improves PyBaMM terminal gain by 142.6% and SWE-Pro success by 6.0 pp at 7.0% and 9.2% lower token cost, respectively. These results demonstrate the efficacy of the proposed approach.</p></details> | <details><summary>25 pa...</summary><p>25 pages; updated experiments, analysis, and figures; code available at https://github.com/SpecRSI-GPR/SpecRSI</p></details> |
| **[Agent Skill Evaluation and Evolution: Frameworks and Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11435v2)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The growth of agent skills has transformed how agentic systems are built, evaluated, and deployed. As skill libraries continue to scale, rigorous evaluation becomes critical to ensuring their utility, quality, and safety in real-world applications. Consequently, the field is undergoing an emerging paradigm shift from isolated skill creation to automated, evaluation-driven skill evolution. In this survey, we systematically examine the landscape of skill evolution and evaluation beyond foundational skill creation. We categorize evolution into four distinct paradigms, spanning execution feedback, trajectory distillation, compression, and reinforcement learning, showing how each element contributes to improving skill utility and reliability. We also provide an analysis of six skill-centric benchmark categories, identifying structural gaps in benchmark coverage, trade-offs, and metric richness to advance skill research. Finally, we identify open directions for building skill ecosystems that are generalizable, efficient, and verifiably safe. The project URL is https://github.com/Cassie07/AgentSkill_Survey</p></details> |  |
| **[Strategist: Evolving Reasoning with Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03618v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A model is only half of an AI agent system. The other half is the inference strategy: the reasoning procedure that turns model calls into a response. Today that second half is written by hand, and the experience of building one strategy never carries to the next. We introduce Strategist, a meta-agent that evolves inference strategies across tasks. Each strategy is written as a blueprint,executable code that other strategies can reuse, and together the blueprints form a library. Given a task and a small development set, Strategist retrieves related blueprints, writes candidate strategies, tests them on real samples, and revises them from the results. Every candidate then enters the library, so later tasks build on earlier discoveries. On 30 benchmarks, Strategist beats the strongest baseline on 24 and matches its average accuracy (74.7% vs.74.0%) at 39% lower inference cost. Its strategies also transfer to unseen tasks and other models without further search. The part of an AI system that is not improved on its own now does.</p></details> | NeurIPS 2026 |
| **[HierFlow: Hierarchical Coupled Dual-Space Search for Automatic Agentic Workflow Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21609v2)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Although structured workflows empower Large Language Models (LLMs) to tackle complex problems, automating their creation is severely hindered by a vast combinatorial search space, frequently resulting in inflexible and resource-heavy offline training dependencies. To address this, we conceptualize workflow generation as an intertwined topology-and-execution search paradigm, where the broader topological layer dictates subtask boundaries and lower-level execution outcomes actively reshape the topology itself. Building on this foundation, we introduce HierFlow, a training-free, test-time hierarchical search architecture that automates agentic workflow design by merging feedback-guided topology adjustments with a fast, MCTS-inspired tree search for sub-workflow optimization. HierFlow maximizes efficiency through an intelligent gating module that selectively triggers execution-level searches based on contextual necessity, a mechanism we further support with an in-depth analysis detailing how varying degrees of cross-task coupling impact the effectiveness of hierarchical splitting. Comprehensive testing across question answering, mathematical reasoning, and code generation benchmarks confirms that HierFlow consistently outperforms strong baselines, delivering an optimal balance of high-quality results and computational efficiency without any additional training overhead.</p></details> |  |
| **[DiabAgent: Toward Agentic Physiological Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03601v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As pretraining scales, medical language models have advanced rapidly in clinical question answering. Their ability to achieve effective physiological control through sustained intervention still lacks systematic quantitative evaluation. Type 1 diabetes provides a concrete setting: impaired insulin secretion creates dependence on exogenous insulin, with dosing continually adjusted using information about meals, exercise, illness, and glucose measurements. We introduce DiabAgent, a research suite for agentic physiological control comprising the Agentic Physiological Control (APC) framework, the DiabetSim simulator, and the SweetBench benchmark. APC controls glucose through medical-device tools, using ongoing observations to infer individual states, accumulate personal experience, and choose treatment actions. We build DiabetSim to simulate different patients, events, and device conditions for scalable virtual testing and training. Using DiabetSim, we construct SweetBench by instantiating 32 scenario designs as 212 glucose-control tasks. With Claude Opus 5.5 and a fixed decision interval of one hour, APC achieves a COGI of 73.5, compared with 71.2 for the open-source closed-loop baseline OpenAPS. Ablations further show that individual adaptation and contextual information improve glucose control. Combining high-frequency rule-based control with lower-frequency agent decisions shows potential for balancing control performance and computational cost. Our work provides a framework, simulator, and evaluation foundation for agents to participate in human physiological control.</p></details> |  |
| **[Four Bridges: Measuring Deception Propensity in a Multi-Agent Game](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03600v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents are increasingly deployed in settings where they interact and coordinate with other agents, yet honesty is mostly measured in single-turn, single-agent benchmarks that pressure the model to deceive. We study deception that emerges in multi-agent interaction without any instruction to deceive. We introduce Kradle, a platform for evaluating AI models, and Four Bridges, an information asymmetry game in which four agents distribute across four rooms: one is the death room, the others hold food worth more if not shared. One agent, the informed agent, knows which room is the death room and can withhold or disclose that information. We evaluate eight frontier models as the informed agent. Deceptive behavior appears in every model and in 73% of all runs (6% to 100% per model), and does not decrease with capability tier within a model family. When the informed agent does disclose, the warning is often challenged, and agents that challenge it enter the death room twice as often as those that do not. Telling all agents that one of them knows the death room collapses deception in six of eight models; telling the informed agent that the room is permanently fatal moves only one. On an open-weight model, an honesty steering vector reduces deception to 0% and adversarial honesty training to 6%. The platform and game provide a testbed for measuring deceptive behavior and comparing interventions.</p></details> | <details><summary>38 pa...</summary><p>38 pages, 14 figures, 16 tables</p></details> |
| **[Spacetime Emergence in the Memory of a Learning Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03596v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We show that the causal structure of spacetime can emerge within the memory of a learning agent performing a quantum information task. We train a model-based reinforcement-learning agent to maximize the quantum mutual information between localized quantum probes interacting with a quantum field in Minkowski spacetime, without directly providing it with information regarding the spacetime metric or causal structure. In learning to accomplish this task, the reinforcement-learning agent constructs an internal predictive model whose planner develops the characteristic null structure of spacetime, allowing us to reconstruct the causal structure of the Minkowski metric with high accuracy. Within this task, spacetime geometry is obtained from an agent's successful predictions and actions, providing a first step toward showing that spacetime concepts emerge from operational quantum experience.</p></details> | 19 pages, 7 figures |
| **[An Agentic AI Workflow for Liver Lesion Segmentation and Structured Radiology Reporting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34599v3)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Despite advances in automated liver lesion segmentation, a gap remains between image-derived predictions and clinically interpretable radiology reports. Bridging this gap requires quantitative measurements that are both reproducible and traceable. We present an evidence-grounded agentic AI framework that connects liver and lesion segmentation with deterministic quantification and structured reporting. TotalSegmentator-based nnU-Net models delineate the liver, Couinaud segments, and lesions, while computational tools extract volumes, dimensions, counts, and anatomical locations. A large language model (LLM) organizes these measurements into a predefined reporting schema, with numerical consistency checks against source measurements. On 51 LiTS cases, the segmentation stage achieved a liver Dice score of 0.947, lesion sensitivity of 75.6%, and positive predictive value of 68.9%. Exploratory screening of 25,494 MERLIN reports identified 10,208 (40.0%) with liver-related abnormalities. By connecting quantitative imaging evidence with agentic report generation, this work takes a step toward human-centered medical AI that prioritizes traceability and clinical relevance.</p></details> |  |
| **[FedReady: Bounded Agents for Federation Formation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03586v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Federated learning (FL) runtimes standardize aggregation and deployment, but each new study still requires federation formation: selecting task-eligible sites and preparing their local data and task code for federated training or analysis. This preparation recurs when the target changes, while raw records and site-private mappings are excluded from cross-site FL messages. We present FedReady, a system that performs federation formation with bounded LLM agents. Agents propose shared contracts, local adapters, and task code; the runtime owns workflow state and accepts artifacts only after checks tied to their digests. Candidate site discovery prioritizes recall; local eligibility verification checks task support before site admission. Once participating sites and artifacts are fixed, conventional federated training or analysis proceeds without readiness agents. Across 39 simulated retinal sites and four tasks, discovery reaches recall 1.00 at precision 0.65; local eligibility verification removes all 17 false positives without losing an eligible site-task pair. The participating sites supply 45,575 task-record instances and complete four 100-round jobs, including one with a runtime-generated contract. Across ten patient cohorts, preparation supports seven patient populations and holds endpoints at three sources as unsupported. Comparison with manually prepared reference populations and analysis (the manual reference) gives a maximum survival-curve difference of 0.001133. We also analyze 19 development failures, targeted classification ablations, and standalone and heterogeneous agent configurations. These results establish the feasibility of task-specific data preparation before ordinary FL training or analysis.</p></details> |  |
| **[TAMPAgent: Agentic Belief-Space Planning for Mobile Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03584v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Open-world robotic mobile manipulation requires acting in physical environments with only partial information. While recent Large Vision-Language Model (LVLM) agents show strong open-world reasoning capabilities, existing architectures that rely on direct tool calling struggle with geometric uncertainty and thus are limited to fully-observable tabletop environments. In contrast, Task and Motion Planning (TAMP) systems can solve long-horizon tasks under partial observability but incur high computational costs and require closed worlds. We propose \textbf{TAMPAgent}, an agentic planning framework that unifies the reasoning of LVLMs with the structured constraint satisfaction of TAMP systems. We endow an LVLM agent with a belief-space planning model operating over an extensible API of perception, planning, and control tools. The agent formulates a partial task plan composed of parameterized observation and execution actions, which is subsequently completed using classical search algorithms. After extracting the plan's geometric constraints, the agent leverages its tool API to bind valid parameter values, satisfying feasibility requirements and producing an executable motion plan. Upon execution, the agent continuously updates its belief history and repeats this closed-loop process by interleaving observation and manipulation actions. Evaluated across complex household mobile manipulation tasks on Rainbow Y1 and Unitree G1 platforms, TAMPAgent significantly outperforms direct tool-calling agentic baselines, demonstrating that integrating TAMP constraint satisfaction into agentic planning loops is essential for unstructured, partially observable environments.</p></details> | <details><summary>Websi...</summary><p>Website link: https://umishra.me/TAMPAgent/</p></details> |
| **[VeriHarm-Bench: Measuring Harmful Success in LLM Coding Agents Verifiably](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03575v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) coding agents increasingly operate with shell access, allowing them to write scripts, move files, alter permissions, and modify system configurations. However, an agent may achieve a user's goal while causing unintended harm, such as deleting unrelated files, weakening access controls, or corrupting data, in response to benign inputs without explicit jailbreaking. This failure mode is particularly important because apparent task success may reassure users that an agent behaved correctly, causing harmful side effects to go unnoticed. We call this harmful success: the agent successfully completes the intended benign task while simultaneously causing unintended harm. Despite its importance, harmful success is difficult to measure because conventional task-success metrics overlook harmful behaviors, while existing safety evaluations are often designed solely to assess harmful outcomes. We introduce VeriHarm, an automatic framework for constructing programmatically verifiable benchmarks that directly measure harmful success. VeriHarm minimally perturbs coding tasks to elicit unintended harmful behavior while maintaining the benignity of the task and preserving the original task objective. It then synthesizes executable harm tests that, together with the original task-correctness tests, evaluate both task accuracy and harmful outcomes. Frontier agents, including GPT-5.6-Sol and Claude-Opus-5, achieve an average task accuracy of 95% on our curated VeriHarm-Bench, yet still struggle to complete tasks safely: their safe success rate, the fraction of tasks completed successfully without triggering the designated harm, averages only 37%. These results suggest that successful task completion can frequently coexist with unintended harm, and that task-success metrics alone may substantially understate the potential safety risks of coding agents.</p></details> | Preprint |
| **[MCP Tool Poisoning in Agentic AI Frameworks: A Cross-Framework Measurement Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03555v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The Model Context Protocol (MCP) lets agentic AI frameworks connect language models to external tool providers, but it carries attacker-controlled content (tool descriptions, schema fields, and response data) into model context without validation. Prior benchmarks measure model compliance with poisoned instructions without confirming real execution. We present the first cross-framework measurement of MCP tool poisoning with real tool execution. A 25-class root-cause taxonomy unifies prior classifications, and the open-source tool mcpvenom drives a 9,720-cell susceptibility matrix across 12 frameworks, 18 models, and 45 attack scenarios, with a dual oracle that confirms exfiltration via canary HTTP receipt. The overall attack success rate is 22.9% (2,113 vulnerable cells). Of these, 64.9% dispatch an attacker tool call, 23.0% contact the attacker endpoint, and 12.1% verifiably exfiltrate a secret. Resistance is model-level: nine core frameworks cluster within 23 to 26% (chi-squared = 3.30, p = 0.91), and no framework implements intentional defense. Incidental framework properties mitigate in specific cases, and a regex description scanner rejects 20 of 45 scenarios at a 7.2% false-positive rate, so metadata scanning alone is a partial control. Model ASR ranges from 0.4% to 49.7%. Claude Opus 4.8 is vulnerable in 8 of 530 cells while issuing 223 explicit refusals. A code-level audit finds no intentional defense across twelve frameworks, and all five coordinated disclosures across four vendors were rejected.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the 19th Workshop on Artificial Intelligence and Security (AISec '26), co-located with ACM CCS 2026, The Hague, Netherlands. 12 pages, 3 figures, 9 tables; 4-page supplement included as ancillary file</p></details> |
| **[Can LLM Agents Be Forward-Deployed Engineers? Requirement Discovery and Delivery for Enterprise Text-to-SQL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03551v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM coding agents can increasingly automate model post-training, but whether they can act as forward-deployed engineer (FDE) agents, discovering customer requirements and turning them into deployment decisions, remains unclear. We study this on a real enterprise text-to-SQL engagement: a simulated customer, grounded in the real use case's requirements, starts with an ambiguous request. An agent must discover acceptance criteria, data permissions, and hardware constraints in the discovery phase, then plan and execute a post-training recipe in the delivery phase. Across 15 models, the strongest discover over 80% of withheld requirements and open-weight models discover 40-50%; in the delivery phase, only 25% of runs across eight coding-agent configurations pass all acceptance criteria. We identify four bottlenecks in this engagement: (1) incomplete discovery, with 40% of withheld requirements missing or misrepresented; (2) unreliable execution; (3) underused compute budgets and a lack of systematic comparisons across base models and post-training methods; and (4) benchmark accuracy does not guarantee production readiness. We suggest human review of discovered requirements, monitoring of the agent's plan during execution, and an explicit experiment-environment specification covering hardware, budget scope, and production checks.</p></details> |  |
| **[Can LLM Agents Improve Imperfect Earth System Model Parameterizations?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03543v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Earth system models (ESMs) rely on parameterizations to represent unresolved processes such as convection, cloud microphysics, and turbulence. However, translating advances in physical understanding into stable, validated parameterizations and integrating them into ESMs often takes years. Agentic AI could accelerate this process and enable more systematic exploration of design choices. Using warm-rain collision-coalescence as a test case, we evaluate agents' ability to synthesize scientific literature, reimplement published bulk parameterizations, and propose new functional forms as differentiable JAX code. We compare conventional parameterizations, data-driven emulators, and agent-generated closures against high-fidelity superdroplet simulations, repeating unattended experiments across ten language models. With expert guidance or iterative feedback, agents produced executable, differentiable, mass-conserving code, although unattended performance depended strongly on the scheme and language model. Iterative feedback lowered median rollout error for eight of ten models. Retuned physical schemes matched or outperformed many learned parameterizations at substantially lower computational cost, but two of 100 unattended agent-generated closures achieved slightly lower rain-mass error than the most accurate retuned scheme on held-out simulations.</p></details> | <details><summary>Sim2S...</summary><p>Sim2Sci Workshop, NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[An Agentic Framework for Automated Power System Studies and Grid Connection Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03534v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Connecting new generation and demand to the grid depends on two scarce skills: checking applications against a large, changing body of regulation, and running the network studies that decide where and how a project connects. We present Grid Agents, an AI system that brings both together, and the methodology we use to evaluate it. The Grid Ontology is its knowledge system. It represents the regulation, standards and engineering knowledge that grid work depends on, makes it searchable, and evolves as the agents learn from use. Every answer drawn from it rests on evidence the agents cite. Grid Studio is an agent system specialised for power-system engineering. It works in its own engineering environment with domain tools and a study framework, draws on the Grid Ontology, and relies on established solvers for every number it reports. In both, engineers make the decisions. We bring eight published power-system benchmark families, previously scattered across separate papers, into one suite of 519 tasks, and we verify not only each answer but the work behind it, by replaying the agent's tool calls. The Grid Ontology cites the right regulatory evidence more often than a general coding agent or retrieval pipelines, leads on LegalBench-RAG and ranks first on the public leaderboards of the Enterprise RAG Challenge and ViDoRe V3. Grid Studio scores highest on the 519 power-system tasks and is the fastest of all the systems we compare, both by a statistically significant margin. With an optimiser in the loop, its reinforcement packages cost 33-67% less on average than those the agent designs alone.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages for the main paper with 6 pages for Appendix</p></details> |
| **[DistDebug-Bench: Can LLM Agents Diagnose Bugs in Distributed Systems?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03523v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents show strong potential in software engineering tasks such as competitive programming and code synthesis. Existing benchmarks, however, largely focus on small-scale settings such as self-contained Python modules, leaving distributed systems, an essential class of production software, under-examined. We introduce DistDebug-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating agents' ability to diagnose complex-system failures at the code level. It curates 120 high-quality real-world bugs from 11 widely used distributed systems, spanning 5 programming languages and 10 failure patterns. Through an extensive evaluation of state-of-the-art LLMs and agents, we identify substantial gaps between current agents and real-world debugging tasks. A recurring gap is tunnel vision due to a lack of explicit reasoning structure and nondeterminism from inconsistent tool-use scope. The benchmark and evaluator are available at https://github.com/OrderLab/distdebug-bench, with agent traces and a leaderboard at https://distdebug.com.</p></details> | <details><summary>NeurI...</summary><p>NeurIPS 2026, Track on Evaluations and Datasets. 41 pages, 10 figures, 24 tables. Benchmark: https://github.com/OrderLab/distdebug-bench. Website: https://distdebug.com</p></details> |
| **[Ask, Don't Equip: Question-Driven Coding Agents for Deep Kernel Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03517v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents for GPU kernel optimization sit at two ends of a structure-freedom tradeoff. Scripted agents fix what the model does at each step, via a learned policy, an optimization menu, or fixed roles, and are sample-efficient where the recipe applies and stall where it does not. Open-ended agents let a self-directed model mutate a population of kernels and pay for the freedom in trials: thousands of evaluations, repeated edits, and wrong candidates. Both respond to hard targets by equipping the agent with more: parsed profiler output, disassembly analysis, retrieval over documentation and libraries, and stores of lessons. We argue that both lack direction, a hypothesis about where the remaining gap is, and that a short question asked at the right moment supplies it without narrowing what the agent may do next. We present KernelSeek, a question-driven agent that makes an existing CUDA kernel faster with no operator-specific knowledge and no faster implementation to imitate: a general-purpose coding agent, a minimal harness that enforces a verifiable goal, and questions at decision points, first from a human, then from a bank the agent applies to itself. On the same task, evaluator, hardware, and week, a tool-rich autonomous agent built in the prevailing style plateaued at 0.50x FlashAttention-3 after 133 model-driven steps; KernelSeek reached 1.13x. It then produced dependency-free FlashAttention forward kernels for Hopper and Blackwell, single CUDA files with no CUTLASS, CuTe, cuBLAS, or cuDNN, correct on all 182 scenarios per architecture and 1.16x FlashAttention-3 and 1.46x FlashAttention-4 (geometric mean). Across 162 human messages and 11.5K agent tool calls, every large gain followed a question that moved the agent to a different layer of the system or rejected a claimed limit, never one that supplied information. We release the kernels, harness, question bank, and all traces.</p></details> |  |
| **[Optimize the Harness, Not the Model: Fitting a Minimal Agent to a Self-Hostable LLM for Investigative Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03434v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Government intelligence teams need LLM agents that can analyze large, messy evidence collections, but security and cost constraints often rule out hosted frontier models and broad, hard-to-audit tool surfaces. We report CLEO: Constrained Loop for Environment Optimization, a method for fitting a minimal agent harness to a fixed, self-hostable LLM. Given an expert-validated dataset, CLEO admits only the tools, guardrails, memory policies, and loop components that improve accuracy while remaining general and auditable. On our held-out dataset, the CLEO harness reaches an 83.8% pass rate, within 1.2 points of the strongest out-of-the-box Claude Code SDK baseline, while being two orders of magnitude smaller.</p></details> | <details><summary>3 pag...</summary><p>3 pages, 1 table. Industry Day talk at CIKM 2026, Rome, 7 November 2026</p></details> |
| **[DeliverBench: Benchmarking AI Agents on Delivering Realistic Multi-Stage Professional Projects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03412v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents are judged by what they deliver, and in professional work what gets delivered is a project, not a task. A project pays off only if the whole package holds, and one weak part can limit the value of the rest. Yet existing benchmarks mostly test agents on one task at a time, leaving a gap between the capability we measure and the outcome that makes agents useful. To address this gap, we introduce DeliverBench, which tests whether agents can carry a project from brief to final delivery. Its 11 projects, spanning financial due diligence, legal contract drafting, and drug safety reporting, are reconstructed with 13 domain experts from real materials and chain 150 subtasks through 2-15 rounds of new materials and client feedback. Because no reference outputs are injected, agents inherit their own errors from one task to the next. Expert rubrics then score every checkpoint as well as the final package. Across seven model-harness systems, even the best system scores only 65.0 out of 100 on final delivery and passes 50.0% of subtasks at a 90-point threshold. The failures are often failures of carrying work forward: in one audit project, all seven systems addressed the review findings, but only one preserved traceability. Together, these results suggest that task-level capability does not simply compose into delivery, which also depends on keeping the work coherent as it evolves.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Reactive to Agentic Resilience: Multi-Timescale AI-RAN Orchestration Under Signaling Storms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03406v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Next-generation open radio access network (Open RAN) and AI-RAN deployments turn the RAN into a disaggregated, compute-aware platform whose service continuity depends on many control loops acting at different timescales. Resilience in this setting is best seen as a lifecycle spanning anticipation, absorption, adaptation, recovery, and evolution. Yet, existing mechanisms remain stage-specific and are rarely coordinated across the control hierarchy. This paper argues that agentic AI offers an orchestration layer that coordinates this lifecycle, turning operator intent into coordinated action while keeping slow language-model reasoning off the fast control path. Using mass-attachment signaling storms as a running study, we build a discrete-event Open RAN attach simulator that captures retry amplification, shared-compute contention, and non-instantaneous provisioning, and we propose a three-tier framework in which an orchestrator at the service management and orchestration layer sets a standing policy, a per-site non-real-time agent judges storms and anticipates scheduled crowds, and a deterministic near-real-time loop enforces every decision. Across experiments, the agentic controller approaches the resilience of a fully provisioned pool of servers at a quarter of its capacity by reading a scheduled event from a calendar and provisioning ahead of it. No reactive weight setting recovers this once the surge arrives. Free-text operator intents ground into control directives at 94% exact match on previously unseen intents, and a single instruction reshapes live behavior end to end. We also quantify the latency, cost, and contention limits that bound it. The supplementary material provides analytical proof of the derived Theorem on anticipation horizon, as well as the subsequent Corollary results on reactive provisioning limits.</p></details> | <details><summary>13 Pa...</summary><p>13 Pages, 9 Figures, 7 Tables. Paper submitted to IEEE for possible publication</p></details> |
| **[Rethinking AI in Medical Imaging: Assumptions, Reality, and Reframing; Towards Physician-Centered Agentic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27428v2)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical imaging has served as primary proving ground for clinical AI, yet a decade of intense research has not translated into proportionate bedside impact. We argue that this gap is not primarily a product of insufficient algorithmic performance, inadequate regulation, or limited explainability. Rather, it reflects a structural misalignment, between how AI systems are designed and evaluated, and how clinical decisions are made. This work identifies 8 interconnected dimensions of this misalignment: 1) the dominance of pixel-only models in a multimodal clinical world; 2) the erosion of physician trust through opaque and inflexible systems; 3) the unfulfilled promise of foundation models in data-sparse medical domains; 4) the persistent bottleneck of non-shareable under-curated datasets; 5) the gap between validated algorithms and deployable clinical platforms; 6) the slow, unmonitored translation of evidence into routine practice; 7) the risk of AI displacing rather than extending physician judgment; and 8) the failure of prediction-centric AI to generate actionable, agentic clinical guidance. For each dimension, we reframe the problem and propose a path forward, culminating in a vision of physician-centered AI solutions with agentic reasoning that extend, rather than replace, clinical judgment.</p></details> | <details><summary>13 pa...</summary><p>13 pages, 3 figures. Accepted to Frontiers Biomed. Tech., 2026</p></details> |
| **[MAS-Guard: Unified Guardrails from Individual Agents to Multi-Agent LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28397v2)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic AI extends large language models (LLMs) from generating responses to planning and executing complex workflows, substantially expanding their utility while exposing applications to unsafe actions, information leakage, and adversarial manipulation. | **[Can We Stop the Backtest Yet? If lifetime allocations sum to at most one, a weighted Ville argument bounds the probability of ever promoting a rule whose declared bounded net score has always had nonpositive conditional mean. The result permits dependent candidates, adaptive births, and indefinite monitoring. We audit the implementation in two settings. A retrospective pseudo-out-of-sample replay contains 24 panel--rule hypotheses, 3,000 shared-sign null paths, injected alternatives, and public ETF and industry data. Repeated unadjusted tests produce a family crossing in 94.5% of null paths, versus 0.0% for PROMOTE; at a 15-bp aligned ETF-component perturbation, PROMOTE has 70.1% target power and median crossing session 2,327. A separate exact mean-zero experiment lets an agent select candidate identities and birth times from past scores; the funded gate has 1.77% false-family crossings across 3,000 programs. The market replay produces zero promotions after costs. The certificate controls historical false promotion under the declared null; it is not a certificate that an earlier edge remains profitable at deployment. AI-use disclosure. Generative AI tools, including OpenAI Codex, assisted manuscript preparation. For this preprint version, Codex assisted with LaTeX editing, author and venue metadata, compilation, and layout checks.</p></details> | <details><summary>9 pag...</summary><p>9 pages, 2 figures. | **[Toward Knowledge-Centric AI: Discovering, Internalizing, and Using Explicit Knowledge Across Agents and Generations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03157v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Knowledge is how understanding accumulates across people and generations: durable records that outlive their producers and that others can check, combine, teach, and correct. | **[From Local Skills to Long-Horizon Web Tasks: Progressive Skill Exploration for LLM Web Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03116v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are driving autonomous agents toward real-world environments, with the Web emerging as one of the most representative interaction scenarios. Across controlled SOC cases and public-SOC validation cases, StateFence lowers attack success and reduces failures that appear only after evidence is poisoned, while preserving task utility and avoiding the indicator loss caused by task-mismatched filtering. ## Drug Design

| **[A Co-Design Approach to Physically-Informed Molecular Docking for Quantum Annealers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.09540v2)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Molecular docking is a crucial step in computer- aided drug design, involving the identification of ligand poses that are physically plausible within a protein pocket. Recent studies have shown that this problem can be formulated as a geometric weighted subgraph isomorphism, which can then be encoded as a QUBO and thus solved on D-Wave quantum annealers. In this work, we adopt a co-design approach, addressing molecular docking at the formulation and encoding levels, and examining how hardware topology affects solution quality on current quantum annealers. We propose new QUBO formulations that extend the state-of-the-art ones by including van der Waals, Coulomb, hydrogen bonding, and hydrophobic interactions. Experimental results show that incorporating physicochemical terms improves the average docking quality by more than 18% compared to the purely geometric baseline on D-Wave quantum annealers with the Pegasus topology, and by up to 29% on those with the Zephyr topology. The choice of topology, along with an improved encoding design, yields significant gains in embedding efficiency and the number of valid solutions. Our designs reduce the number of physical qubits required by up to 14.5% and decrease the chain break fraction by an order of magnitude, thereby enhancing confidence in the resulting solutions.</p></details> | 7 pages, 3 figures |
| **[Latent Style-based Quantum Wasserstein GAN for Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.22399v2)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The development of new drugs is a time-consuming and expensive process, with average costs estimated to be up to around $2.5 billion. The first step in this process is the design of the new drug, for which de novo drug design, assisted by artificial intelligence, has blossomed in recent years and has revolutionized the field. In particular, generative artificial intelligence has delivered promising results in drug discovery and development, reducing costs and time to solution. However, classical generative models, such as generative adversarial networks (GANs), can be difficult to train and are prone to mode collapse. Quantum computing may be an avenue to overcome some of these issues, providing compact models with far fewer trainable parameters, thereby enhancing the generalizability of GANs. We propose a new latent style-based quantum Wasserstein GAN (QGAN) for drug design, combining a variational autoencoder that maps molecular structures into a latent space with a hybrid quantum GAN. Our pipeline is benchmarked against classical models of the industry-relevant MOSES suite. Style-based noise reuploading at every rotational gate and a Wasserstein gradient penalty are used to improve trainability and to mitigate mode collapse. Training and large-scale sampling with circuits of up to 15 qubits are performed on noiseless quantum simulators to validate our architecture. We present an inference-only demonstration on an IBM Heron quantum computer using 5 qubits, sampling 2,500 new molecules, to investigate quantum noise effects. Our default QGAN, using 110 trainable generator parameters, reaches statistically comparable overall performance and statistically significant gains on selected drug-relevant metrics relative to both the MOSES classical latent GAN baseline, which contains about 700,000 trainable parameters for the generator, and a classical latent GAN with matched generator capacity.</p></details> | <details><summary>Main ...</summary><p>Main part: 26 pages, 11 figures, 6 tables. | **[Beyond SBDD: Geometric Deep Learning in Polypharmacology and Multi-target Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20550v1)** | 2026-07-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The traditional "one drug, one target" paradigm of structure-based drug design (SBDD) frequently proves inadequate for treating multifactorial diseases such as cancer and neurodegenerative disorders, owing to compensatory signaling pathways and the emergence of drug resistance. While polypharmacology offers a synergistic therapeutic strategy, the rational design of ligands capable of simultaneously satisfying the geometric constraints imposed by multiple targets remains a major computational bottleneck. This review positions geometric deep learning (GDL) as a powerful integrative approach to overcome these limitations. We systematically survey GDL architectures ranging from invariant graph neural networks to SE(3)-equivariant diffusion models that harness non-Euclidean molecular data to capture intrinsic three-dimensional (3D) structural interdependencies. We critically analyze GDL applications across three core dimensions, including the characterization of shared binding pockets via geometric embeddings, multi-target bioactivity prediction through heterogeneous graph fusion, and de novo generation of dual-target ligands. Particular emphasis is placed on emerging structure-conditioned generative algorithms that integrate diffusion models with reinforcement learning to autonomously resolve complex geometric conflicts between competing binding sites. Furthermore, we evaluate the pivotal role of multimodal omics integration and specialized geometric benchmarking infrastructures in validating these models. By synthesizing these methodological advances, this review elucidates the paradigm shift in drug discovery from serendipitous exploration to rational, structure-driven polypharmacological molecular engineering, thereby providing a clear, structured guide for navigating the complexities of next-generation therapeutics.</p></details> |  |
| **[Generating Developable 3D Molecules via Pocket-Conditioned Diffusion and Property-Aware Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12349v1)** | 2026-07-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Drug discovery and development is time-consuming and resource-intensive, motivating computational approaches such as diffusion models for de novo drug design. Many such models follow the structure-based drug design (SBDD) paradigm, generating molecules to fit a target binding pocket. However, existing diffusion-based SBDD methods typically couple pocket and ligand representation learning, model interactions only at the atom level, and prioritize binding affinity over other developability properties. Here, we introduce conDitar-dev, a conditional diffusion-based SBDD framework for generating ligands with strong binding affinities and favorable ADMET properties. It consists of three modules: msPRL, a pretrained multi-scale pocket representation learning module; conDitar, a pocket-conditioned diffusion model guided by msPRL representations; and paOPT, a generation-time method for optimizing ligand developability. On a newly curated benchmark of human disease targets, conDitar outperforms state-of-the-art SBDD baselines, achieving an average binding score of -8.85 kcal/mol. Across five ADMET properties, conDitar-dev improves performance by up to 73% over conDitar. To further validate the abilities of conDitar-dev to generate developable molecules, we have applied it to two validated druggable targets: programmed death-ligand 1 (PD-L1) and colony-stimulating factor 1 receptor (CSF1R) proteins. Top-ranked generatively designed molecules and their analogs have been experimentally synthesized and biologically tested. Two molecules generated directly by conDitar-dev for PD-L1 exhibited SPR-derived $K_D$ values of 3.49 and 3.75 $μ$M, respectively. Hit expansion based on conDitar-dev-designed molecules identified selective CSF1R inhibitors with IC$_{50}$ values as low as 200 nM, while also uncovering opportunities for drug repositioning.</p></details> |  |
| **[DrugGen 2: A disease-aware language model for enhancing drug discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08404v1)** | 2026-07-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Current computational approaches for drug design typically focus on generating molecules conditioned on specific targets or general molecular properties, often neglecting the influence of disease context on target behavior and therapeutic outcomes. DrugGen-2 was developed by fine-tuning a pre-trained GPT-2 model on a curated dataset of approved drugs linked to their diseases and targets, using a two-step strategy of supervised fine-tuning followed by reinforcement learning via group relative policy optimization (GRPO). This process was guided by reward functions optimizing for chemical validity, novelty, diversity, and high predicted binding affinity. When evaluated on five protein targets relevant to diabetic nephropathy, DrugGen-2 significantly outperformed baseline models (DrugGPT and DrugGen). It demonstrated a superior capacity to generate unique molecules, exhibited greater structural similarity to approved drugs, and achieved improved predicted binding affinities across all targets. Molecular docking analyses further supported these findings, identifying candidate ligands with strong binding potential, including compounds with predicted affinities (-9.917, -9.485, and -9.367) exceeding those of reference drugs such as enalapril for angiotensin-converting enzyme (-8.283). By integrating disease-specific context into molecular generation, DrugGen-2 advances AI-assisted drug discovery, offering a powerful tool for de novo design and drug repurposing that accounts for the complex interplay between diseases and molecular targets.</p></details> | <details><summary>15 pa...</summary><p>15 pages, 2 figures, 1 table, and 4 supplementary files. To use the model, see https://github.com/alimotahharynia/DrugGen-2</p></details> |
| **[URSA: Chemistry-Aware Benchmark for Utilitarian Retrosynthesis Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04688v1)** | 2026-07-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Synthesis planning aiming to find pathways of reactions for a target molecule is one of the most important and challenging tasks in drug discovery. In the current study, we are introducing the URSA (Utilitarian RetroSynthesis Assessment) evaluation framework that provides the opportunity to benchmark the synthetic routes not only from a formal perspective, such as convergence to commercially available starting materials, but also from a chemical plausibility perspective, mimicking the way expert chemists evaluate the reactions and routes. The study covers a comprehensive evaluation of both conventional end-to-end retrosynthesis solutions and LLMs for the synthesis planning task on a set of novel, diverse target molecules with undisclosed synthetic routes, which represent realistic tasks in the daily drug design routine. We find that while LLMs can support high-level strategic planning, they currently underperform specialized retrosynthesis models in reliably solving synthesis planning tasks.</p></details> |  |
| **[SynLaD: Latent Diffusion for Generating Synthesizable Molecules Conditioned on 3D Pharmacophore Profiles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01105v1)** | 2026-07-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present SynLaD, a latent diffusion framework for small-molecule generation that unifies ligand-based drug design objectives (what to make) with synthetic accessibility (how to make it). We propose a multi-modal flow DPO co-modeling strategy that simultaneously aligns discrete and continuous modalities, leading to consistent improvements across multiple evaluation metrics. The source code for MolFORM is publicly available at https://github.com/huang3170/MolForm.git.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to ICML 2025 genbio workshop</p></details> |
| **[Sesame: Structure-Aware Molecular Generation via Spatial Density-Map Conditioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23856v2)** | 2026-06-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generative molecular models for drug design are a promising direction with much active research. In addition to this module, we also introduce a diffusion framework for joint denoising of atom types, bond types, and positions, along with a trajectory finetuning scheme that trains on the model's own sampling rollouts to improve generation quality. Sesame is trained on a large corpus of ligand-only and protein-ligand datasets.</p></details> | <details><summary>24 pa...</summary><p>24 pages, 4 figures, preprint</p></details> |
| **[Contrastive Geometric Learning Unlocks Unified Structure- and Ligand-Based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.09693v3)** | 2026-06-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Structure-based and ligand-based computational drug design have traditionally relied on disjoint data sources and modeling assumptions, limiting their joint use at scale. Across diverse benchmarks, ConGLUDe achieves competitive zero-shot virtual screening performance, substantially outperforms existing methods on a challenging target fishing task, and demonstrates state-of-the-art ligand-conditioned pocket selection. | **[Sampling Out-of-Distribution Chemical Spaces via Bayesian Flow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.11439v6)** | 2026-06-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generating novel molecules with higher properties than the training space, namely the out-of-distribution generation, is important for de novo drug design. | **[ShallowBench: Benchmarking Generative Drug Design Models on Shallow-Pocket Targets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06717v1)** | 2026-06-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While generative AI models have demonstrated remarkable success in structure-based drug design, they predominantly rely on deep binding pockets and struggle to sample effective ligands for challenging low-pocketability targets, such as the historically "undruggable" oncology targets KRAS and MYC. Based on this, we introduce EDMolGPT, a decoder-only autoregressive framework that generates molecules from low-resolution ED point clouds. By grounding generation in physically meaningful density signals, EDMolGPT mitigates structural bias and produces molecules with 3D conformations. Evaluations on 101 biological targets verify the effectiveness. | **[On the Collapse of Generative Paths: A Criterion and Correction for Diffusion Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.10339v2)** | 2026-06-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Inference-time steering adapts pretrained diffusion and flow models to new tasks without retraining, often utilizing ratio-of-densities constructions that reweight time-indexed marginals with fixed exponents. We identify Marginal Path Collapse, a failure mode in which the intermediate density defined by such compositions becomes non-normalizable despite valid endpoints. This collapse can arise when composing heterogeneous experts trained with mismatched noise schedules (and/or negative exponents / partial supports). To address this, we provide (i) a sharp sufficient Path Existence Criterion that characterizes when the composed intermediate densities are mathematically well-defined, and (ii) Adaptive Path Correction with Exponents (ACE), which generalizes Feynman-Kac steering to support time-varying exponents. Our analysis reveals that ACE controls the quantile radius of the intermediate distributions, providing a theoretical mechanism for path stabilization observed in experiments. On flexible-pose scaffold decoration, a drug design task composed of de-novo, conformer, and protein-conditioned experts, ACE prevents collapse and significantly outperforms constant-exponent baselines. Furthermore, ACE improves attribute success rates in compositional image generation, establishing it as a general framework for compositional sampling. Project Page: https://ziseoklee.github.io/projects/ACE/</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to ICML 2026</p></details> |

We argue that a critical but overlooked degree of freedom lies in how these marginal processes are temporally coupled during training and generation, where inappropriate coupling can introduce high-variance supervision and inconsistent intermediate states, affecting modality consistency. To address this, we introduce GeoCoupling, a systematic framework that optimizes for temporal couplings between heterogeneous modalities. Empirical results across structure-based drug design and unconditional protein design demonstrate the learned couplings consistently outperform synchronous and randomly coupled baselines, yielding biomolecules with improved physical validity and diversity.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to ICML 2026</p></details> |

By optimizing a fixed threshold-aware reward, FTDiff effectively guides the model to produce valid, diverse, and high- quality molecules that balance multiple drug design objectives. In Symbolic Neural Generators (SNGs), symbolic learners examine logical specifications of feasible data from a small set of instances -- sometimes just one. Each specification in turn constrains the conditional information supplied to a neural-based generator, which rejects any instance violating the symbolic specification. Like other neurosymbolic approaches, SNG exploits the complementary strengths of symbolic and neural methods. The outcome of an SNG is a pair $(H, X)$, where $H$ is a symbolic description of feasible instances constructed from data, and $X$ a set of generated new instances that satisfy the description. We introduce a semantics for such systems, based on the construction of appropriate base and fibre partially-ordered sets combined into an overall partial order. We implement an SNG combining a restricted form of Inductive Logic Programming (ILP) with a large language model (LLM) and evaluate it on early-stage drug design. Our main interest is the description and the set of potential inhibitor molecules generated by the SNG. On benchmark problems -- where drug targets are well understood -- SNG performance is statistically comparable to state-of-the-art methods. Case studies validate this: selective CK2$α$ ligand generation against CLK3 shows significant correlation between predicted and quantum-mechanical binding energies. Scaffold elaboration on ER$α$, TYK2, and BACE1 demonstrates strong agreement between predicted affinities and QM calculations while confirming geometric fidelity. | **[MolPIF: A Parameter Interpolation Flow Model for Molecule Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.13762v4)** | 2026-05-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Motivation: Structure-based drug design (SBDD) has advanced with deep generative models, but bridging the gap between continuous atomic coordinates and discrete atom types remains a challenge. This lack of a consistent framework limits generative models' ability to capture the geometric and chemical structure of protein-ligand complexes. Unlike traditional flow models that operate in sample space, MolPIF interpolates between distributions in the parameter space, theoretically recovering Wasserstein-2 optimal transport for continuous coordinates and establishing Fisher-Rao geodesics for discrete atom types. We further incorporate a geometry-enhanced learning strategy to improve the capture of atomic contexts. Extensive evaluations on the CrossDocked2020 dataset demonstrate that MolPIF outperforms baselines in binding affinity, chemical validity, geometric fidelity and chemical space coverage. Additionally, MolPIF exhibits versatility in lead optimization and offers flexible prior distribution selection (such as Laplace), establishing a robust paradigm for SBDD. Supplementary information: Supplementary data are available at Bioinformatics.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to Bioinformatics</p></details> |

Crucially, by leveraging these experimental restraints, AIMS-Fold achieves higher accuracy on challenging induced proximity targets than purely computational, unguided state-of-the-art models like Boltz-2. | **[EvoEGF-Mol: Evolving Exponential Geodesic Flow for Structure-based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.22466v2)** | 2026-05-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Structure-Based Drug Design (SBDD) aims to discover bioactive ligands. | **[SMDD-Bench: Can LLMs Solve Real-World Small Molecule Drug Design Tasks?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.21740v2)** | 2026-05-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents have incredible potential for scientific discovery applications. | **[SciCore-Mol: Augmenting Large Language Models with Pluggable Molecular Cognition Modules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22287v1)** | 2026-05-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) are central to the one-for-all intelligent paradigm, but they face a fundamental challenge when dealing with heterogeneous scientific data such as molecules: the inherent gap between discrete linguistic symbols and topological molecular or continuous reaction data leads to significant information loss and semantic noise in text-based reasoning. Preprint</p></details> |
| **[Harnessing AtomisticSkills for Agentic Atomistic Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24002v1)** | 2026-05-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Computational materials science and chemistry span vast knowledge domains and fractured software ecosystems. By hierarchically decomposing scientific workflows into agent skills and tools, AtomisticSkills provides agents with modular, extensible, and plug-and-play research capabilities. The framework integrates more than 100 human-curated multidisciplinary skills, including database access, thermodynamics and kinetics modeling, and diverse simulation engines employing machine learning interatomic potentials (MLIPs) and density functional theory (DFT). We validate its functional coverage against scientific literature and demonstrate robust orchestration capabilities across diverse scientific campaigns: generative design of Li-ion solid-state electrolytes, high-throughput screening of metal-organic frameworks for CO2 capture, autonomous MLIP benchmarking and fine-tuning, multi-stage structure-based virtual screening for drug design, multimodal X-ray diffraction pattern analysis, and screening of Fe-oxide catalysts for oxygen evolution reaction. | **[Generative Pseudo-Force Fields for Molecular Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.19050v1)** | 2026-05-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generating stable molecular conformations typically forces a tradeoff between the physical realism of energy-based relaxation and the sampling efficiency of data-driven generative models. While machine learning force fields (MLFFs) can sample stable conformations by relaxing molecular geometries according to physical forces, they require costly ab-initio training data. Conversely, diffusion models (DMs) learn from equilibrium data alone but are dependent on noise schedules and time-step conditioning. In this work, we propose generative pseudo-force fields (GPFFs) to bridge these paradigms by training an MLFF on a quadratic pseudo-potential energy surface relative to reference equilibrium structures. Because no ab-initio calculations are required for the perturbed geometries, non-equilibrium training data can be generated on the fly by perturbing the equilibria with Gaussian noise. We show that GPFFs constitute a time-step-agnostic variant of variance exploding DMs: the score comes from the predicted pseudo-forces but because force magnitudes implicitly encode the noise level, no time-step conditioning is needed. Our GPFF can hence be used as a drop-in replacement in standard diffusion sampling (ancestral, Heun) but also facilitates more efficient, adaptive variants and an MLFF inspired direct denoising scheme. Our proposed sampling algorithms support arbitrary structural priors and geometric constraints. On QM9, GPFF has 100 % validity at 256 neural function evaluations (NFE) and over 50 % at just 6 NFE, outperforming diffusion baselines across all samplers. Combined with custom priors, we showcase the fast and accurate generation process of our method in a molecular editor for a drug design setting, where a molecule is generated in real time.</p></details> |  |
| **[Fine-tuning Pocket-Aware Diffusion Models via Denoising Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17693v1)** | 2026-05-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Structure-based drug design has been accelerated by pocket-aware 3D generative models, yet most methods primarily fit the training distribution and may fall short of satisfying multiple properties required in real-world therapeutic drug discovery. | **[Reading the Cell, Designing the Cure: Perturbation-Conditioned Molecular Diffusion for Function-Oriented Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.15243v1)** | 2026-05-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When reliable target structures are unavailable at scale or phenotypes arise from dysregulated pathways, transcriptomic perturbations provide a system-level functional readout for drug action. | **[In-Context Multi-Objective Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.11114v2)** | 2026-05-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Balancing competing objectives is omnipresent across disciplines, from drug design to autonomous systems. Multi-objective Bayesian optimization is a promising solution for such expensive, black-box problems: it fits probabilistic surrogates and selects new designs via an acquisition function that balances exploration and exploitation. In practice, it requires tailored choices of surrogate and acquisition that rarely transfer to the next problem, is myopic when multi-step planning is often required, and adds refitting overhead, particularly in parallel or time-sensitive loops. We present TAMO, a fully amortized, universal policy for multi-objective black-box optimization. TAMO uses a transformer architecture that operates across varying input and objective dimensions, enabling pretraining on diverse corpora and transfer to new problems without retraining: at test time, the pretrained model proposes the next design with a single forward pass. We pretrain the policy with reinforcement learning to maximize cumulative hypervolume improvement over full trajectories, conditioning on the entire query history to approximate the Pareto frontier. Across synthetic benchmarks and real tasks, TAMO produces fast proposals, reducing proposal time by 50-1000x versus alternatives while matching or improving Pareto quality under tight evaluation budgets. These results show that transformers can perform multi-objective optimization entirely in-context, eliminating per-task surrogate fitting and acquisition engineering, and open a path to foundation-style, plug-and-play optimizers for scientific discovery workflows.</p></details> |  |

| **[Distributional Priors Guided Diffusion for Generating 3D Molecules in Low Data Regimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.00962v2)** | 2026-03-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Can we train a 3D molecule generator using data from dense regions to generate samples in sparse regions? ## multi-objective optimization
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[NeuroPareto: Calibrated Acquisition for Costly Many-Goal Search in Vast Parameter Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.03901v7)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The pursuit of optimal trade-offs in high-dimensional search spaces under stringent computational constraints poses a fundamental challenge for contemporary multi-objective optimization. We develop NeuroPareto, a cohesive architecture that integrates rank-centric filtering, uncertainty disentanglement, and history-conditioned acquisition strategies to navigate complex objective landscapes. A calibrated Bayesian classifier estimates epistemic uncertainty across non-domination tiers, enabling rapid generation of high-quality candidates with minimal evaluation cost. Deep Gaussian Process surrogates further separate predictive uncertainty into reducible and irreducible components, providing refined predictive means and risk-aware signals for downstream selection. A lightweight acquisition network, trained online from historical hypervolume improvements, guides expensive evaluations toward regions balancing convergence and diversity. With hierarchical screening and amortized surrogate updates, the method maintains accuracy while keeping computational overhead low. Experiments on DTLZ and ZDT suites and a subsurface energy extraction task show that NeuroPareto consistently outperforms classifier-enhanced and surrogate-assisted baselines in Pareto proximity and hypervolume.</p></details> | 39 pages, 19 figures |
| **[Diffusion-based Evolutionary Optimization for 3D Multi-Objective Molecular Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11037v6)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>De novo 3D molecular design with multiple properties and fragment-retention constraints is formulated as a constrained multi-objective optimization problem. DEMO performs crossover and mutation on intermediate diffusion states through an Evolutionary-Guided Diffusion (EGD) operator, combines 2D topology and 3D geometry in Structure-Aware Environmental Selection (SAES) to preserve structural diversity, and uses three cooperating populations to coordinate structural exploration, fragment-constraint satisfaction, and property optimization. Experimental results demonstrate that EGD outperforms task-specific conditional generators in property targeting; SAES preserves structural diversity while retaining competitive hypervolume in multi-objective optimization; and DEMO improves objective convergence and generates diverse feasible molecules under fragment constraints. These results show how evolutionary search can reuse existing diffusion models for diverse molecular design requirements.</p></details> |  |

To address this gap, we propose EvoPS (Evolutionary Patch Selection), a novel framework that formulates patch selection as a multi-objective optimization problem and leverages an evolutionary search to simultaneously minimize the number of selected patch embeddings and maximize the performance of a downstream similarity search task, generating a Pareto front of optimal trade-off solutions. The results demonstrate that EvoPS can reduce the required number of training patch embeddings by over 90% while consistently maintaining or even improving the final classification F1-score compared to a baseline that uses all available patches' embeddings selected through a standard extraction pipeline. On this problem class, we analyze the runtime of GSEMO and the widely used NSGA-II. Our results show that allowing a dynamic population size for NSGA-II can lead to a moderate improvement, yielding a super-constant speedup for $\ell \in ω(\log(n)) \cap o(\sqrt{n \log(n)})$ in terms of fitness evaluations. | **[LOADBench: A Large Open Analog Dataset and Benchmark for Machine Learning in IC Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21591v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Machine learning (ML) for analog IC design is constrained by limited open data and the absence of standardized evaluation: results reported in different papers are typically obtained on bespoke, often closed, datasets and with ad-hoc metrics, leaving comparisons across methods unreliable. By exploiting the problem structure, we develop an efficient iterative algorithm with closed-form updates to solve the non-convex optimization problem with low complexity. Numerical results are provided to evaluate the performance of the proposed tri-HBF architecture in various setups. The results demonstrate that the proposed tri-HBF architecture can achieve significant improvement in energy efficiency (EE) compared to the conventional hybrid MIMO and DMA-only configurations, at the cost of minor degradations in sum rate and sensing mutual information.</p></details> | <details><summary>13 pa...</summary><p>13 pages, journal extention of our previous conference work</p></details> |
| **[Quantum Approximate Multi-Objective Optimization in Routing Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00168v1)** | 2026-09-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-objective optimization (MOO) problems are common in logistics, where routing decisions must balance conflicting objectives such as travel distance, delivery time, and operational risk. However, its effectiveness has only been demonstrated on proof-of-concept instances tailored to quantum hardware connectivity. In this work, we evaluate the applicability of this strategy to realistic routing problems. We formulate the Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP) and Vehicle Routing Problem (VRP) as Quadratic Unconstrained Binary Optimization (QUBO) models, reduce them to MAX-CUT, and assess the parameter-transfer framework under conditions matching the original study. Validation is performed through classical simulations and experiments on IBM quantum hardware. The resulting Pareto fronts are compared with those obtained using an adapted classical ε-constraint method, using hypervolume as the primary quality metric. Results indicate that parameter transfer remains effective, frequently achieving higher hypervolume and often finding competitive solutions earlier. However, performance depends on problem structure. The approach is consistently effective for TSP instances but less stable for the more constrained VRP, suggesting that QAOA parameter transferability decreases as the optimization landscape becomes more complex. | **[DQN-Scheduler: A Multi-Objective Optimization Framework for Scheduling Microservices in Cloud Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14117v1)** | 2026-09-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cloud computing has emerged as an information technology solution, providing software and infrastructure solutions for companies and individuals. The pay-as-you-go approach has increased demands for the cloud. The massive range of resources, the variety of services, and flexible pricing grab attention. Additionally, container technology has boosted the popularity of microservices by offering a platform for this type of architecture. Containers and microservices improve the flexibility and scalability of cloud applications. There are two primary types of microservices: batch and online services, with the majority of applications falling into the online service category. In this study, we introduce the DQN-Scheduler, a novel reinforcement learning-based agent designed to optimize microservice scheduling in cloud environments. Our approach aims to optimize multiple scheduling objectives simultaneously, such as resource utilization, load balancing, latency, reliability, and availability. To our knowledge, this is the first framework to address all these objectives simultaneously. The DQN-Scheduler was tested against benchmark algorithms in the field. The experimental results demonstrate that the DQN-Scheduler outperforms benchmark algorithms.</p></details> |  |

Its non-linear operations, such as softmax, normalization, and activation, must be replaced with polynomial approximations that the CKKS scheme supports, and the depth of these approximations dominates inference cost. Existing FHE Transformers use hand-tuned approximation settings, such as iteration count and polynomial degree, applied uniformly across layers, models, and tasks. We present ATLAS, a training-free framework that automates this search by treating each layer's approximation setting as a multi-objective optimization over latency and accuracy. ## DNA
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Searching for related contributors to a DNA mixture using single cell analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01782v1)** | 2026-11-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We propose a staged process for assessing the presence of related individuals in an autosomal STR DNA mixture sample, when it is analysed using a single cell pipeline analysis (as opposed to a bulk mixture analysis). It is applied to create random admixtures used in a relatedness search for pairs of individuals to illustrate the usefulness of the method.</p></details> | 26 pages, 14 tables |
| **[IsoAlign: Isoform-Aware DNA-Protein Alignment via Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00241v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Genomic foundation models (GFMs) have emerged as a powerful paradigm for learning DNA sequence representations, yet existing self-supervised pretraining strategies overlook alternative splicing and underperform compared to specialized models. To address this gap, we introduce IsoAlign, a multimodal framework that grounds genomic pretraining in isoform-aware DNA-protein alignment to learn biologically meaningful representations. IsoAlign aligns transcripts to their corresponding protein isoforms, capturing both coding sequences and broader regulatory contexts, using two Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPA): DNA-Protein JEPA (DP-JEPA), which predicts protein representations from DNA using contrastive alignment, and Isoform-Masked JEPA (IM-JEPA), which models relationships among alternative isoforms of the same gene through masked protein prediction. We evaluate the learned DNA representations of these architectures across a multi-metric evaluation suite spanning tasks, including the prediction of pathogenicity, fitness, gene expression, and TF binding. Our results demonstrate that isoform-aware cross-modal alignment using JEPA significantly improves functional representations and downstream performance, establishing a promising direction for GFM pretraining.</p></details> |  |
| **[Locality-Controlled Out-of-Distribution Guidance for Robust Regulatory DNA Sequence Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41736v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Designing regulatory DNA sequences for targeted gene-expression control is an important challenge. Recent approaches frame this task as property-conditioned generation, where diffusion models trained on natural sequences are guided toward high predicted activity through reward-based fine-tuning or sampling-time guidance. We show that single-reward evaluation can obscure reward-hacking behavior. By decomposing evaluation into property, novelty, and plausibility, we identify an empirically underexplored region of the evaluation space in which all three objectives are satisfied, but no existing baseline succeeds. We postulate that this gap arises because prior methods treat guidance primarily as a matter of magnitude. In regulatory DNA, however, sequence grammar is spatially nonuniform: motif positions are sensitive to perturbation, while background regions allow more exploration. We therefore propose LOCOGEN by reformulating guidance as a problem of locality. LOCOGEN learns an invariant motif mask through sequence-level invariance learning to protect motif positions, while applying OOD damping only to background positions. It operates at sampling time on a frozen diffusion backbone, without retraining the backbone. Across HepG2, K562, and SK-N-SH, LOCOGEN reaches this previously underexplored region of the property-novelty-plausibility space. Code is available at https://github.com/Lotfollahi-lab/LocoGen.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |

If the joint SIR $J_t$ of a fixed number $t$ of reads under independent, uniformly distributed input nucleotides exceeds $r_\star$, the ensemble-average full extended-word block error is $O(1/n)$, and deterministic graph-offset sequences attain the same limiting rate and message-average error bound. The channel permits arbitrarily many insertions and unbounded drift, with no alignment supplied to the receiver.
