| **[Peak-DNA: Recurrent Morphology in Global Narrative Propagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36571v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Global narrative streams exhibit noisy, fragmented, and asynchronous amplification bursts, making direct waveform comparison unreliable for recurrence analysis. These findings suggest a measurable morphology layer beyond semantic content or anomaly intensity, providing an initial structural foundation for recurrence-aware narrative monitoring, propagation family discovery, and longitudinal Narrative Genome analysis.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages. | **[ACOR: incremental evidence accumulation towards real-time DNA storage reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36500v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>DNA offers high-density, durable storage by encoding digital information into synthetic strands and retrieving it through sequencing. Yet, accurately characterizing this heterogeneity from bulk molecular profiles remains challenging because observed signals arise from mixtures of multiple cell populations. Cell deconvolution aim to recover the relative abundance of constituent cell types from such heterogeneous measurements, but most existing approaches implicitly rely on restrictive assumptions on residual errors, including independence, homoscedasticity, and normality. These assumptions are rarely satisfied in omics data, which are inherently bounded and overdispersed. In this work, we show that whole-genome cell-type specific DNA methylation profiles exhibit latent group structures that can substantially impair deconvolution accuracy when ignored. We therefore propose a mixture of non-negative Beta regression models estimated through an Expectation-Maximization algorithm for DNA methylation rates. Our framework naturally incorporates a feature selection mechanism through mixture component identification, making component selection a critical step of the inference procedure. We further propose a dedicated criterion for component selection and assess the performance of the approach through an extensive comparative study across several in vitro benchmark datasets. Our results demonstrate that deconvolution accuracy is highly sensitive to latent component structure and show that explicitly modeling this heterogeneity yields substantial improvements over standard whole-genome deconvolution strategies. Altogether, this work establishes mixture modeling of DNA methylation data as a powerful new direction for robust and accurate cell deconvolution.</p></details> |  |

## LLM
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Tracing the Scientific Discovery Capacity of LLMs to Their Training Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03617v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) show growing promise for scientific discovery, yet basic questions remain about what enables reliable discovery. How can we trace the scientific discovery capacity of LLMs to their training data? We study this question as one of missing probability mass and develop a theoretical account in which monofacts-the facts observed once during training-set the discovery capacity. In materials science and regulatory genomics, we directly manipulate monofact rates in training sets and find that more monofacts consistently yield more discoveries across two open-weight model families, sampling strategies, and reasoning budgets. Further experiments show the relationship cannot be explained by training-set diversity alone and can transfer across scientific domains when paired with in-domain data. Together, our results identify monofact rate in training data as a key factor governing discoveries in language models.</p></details> |  |
| **[CoSpaDi: Compressing LLMs via Calibration-Guided Sparse Dictionary Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.22075v8)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Post-training LLM compression often relies on low-rank approximations, which force all columns of a projection matrix to share a single low-dimensional subspace. We propose CoSpaDi, a training-free compression framework that replaces this single-subspace assumption with a union-of-subspaces model via sparse dictionary learning. CoSpaDi factorizes each weight matrix into a dense dictionary and column-sparse coefficients, allowing different columns to select different subsets of dictionary atoms at the same storage budget. To preserve model behavior, we use calibration activations to transform functional reconstruction into a standard dictionary learning problem. Across LLaMA and Qwen models, CoSpaDi improves accuracy-compression and perplexity-compression trade-offs over SVD-based and structured pruning baselines at 20-40\% compression ratios, while naturally supporting sparse-dense computation and post-training quantization of sparse coefficients.</p></details> | <details><summary>Paper...</summary><p>Paper was accepted to the Findings of ACL: EMNLP 2026</p></details> |
| **[VeriHarm-Bench: Measuring Harmful Success in LLM Coding Agents Verifiably](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03575v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) coding agents increasingly operate with shell access, allowing them to write scripts, move files, alter permissions, and modify system configurations. However, an agent may achieve a user's goal while causing unintended harm, such as deleting unrelated files, weakening access controls, or corrupting data, in response to benign inputs without explicit jailbreaking. This failure mode is particularly important because apparent task success may reassure users that an agent behaved correctly, causing harmful side effects to go unnoticed. We call this harmful success: the agent successfully completes the intended benign task while simultaneously causing unintended harm. Despite its importance, harmful success is difficult to measure because conventional task-success metrics overlook harmful behaviors, while existing safety evaluations are often designed solely to assess harmful outcomes. We introduce VeriHarm, an automatic framework for constructing programmatically verifiable benchmarks that directly measure harmful success. VeriHarm minimally perturbs coding tasks to elicit unintended harmful behavior while maintaining the benignity of the task and preserving the original task objective. It then synthesizes executable harm tests that, together with the original task-correctness tests, evaluate both task accuracy and harmful outcomes. Frontier agents, including GPT-5.6-Sol and Claude-Opus-5, achieve an average task accuracy of 95% on our curated VeriHarm-Bench, yet still struggle to complete tasks safely: their safe success rate, the fraction of tasks completed successfully without triggering the designated harm, averages only 37%. These results suggest that successful task completion can frequently coexist with unintended harm, and that task-success metrics alone may substantially understate the potential safety risks of coding agents.</p></details> | Preprint |
| **[TSNBench: Benchmarking LLM Proficiency in Time-Sensitive Networking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09481v2)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present TSNBench, the first benchmark for evaluating large language model (LLM) proficiency in Time-Sensitive Networking (TSN), a suite of IEEE 802.1 standards for deterministic communication with bounded latency in safety-critical domains such as autonomous vehicles, aviation, defense, and industrial automation. While LLMs have been extensively evaluated on general knowledge tasks, their capabilities in safety-critical networking domains remain largely unexplored. TSNBench comprises 939 expert-validated multiple-choice questions (MCQs) covering diverse TSN mechanisms, along with 100 open-ended Worst-Case Delay (WCD) computation tasks for each of Credit-Based Shaper (CBS) and Cyclic Queuing and Forwarding (CQF), across varying topologies and traffic flows. The ground-truth (GT) WCD values are computed using a verified Network Calculus (NC) solver for CBS and closed-form upper bounds for CQF. We evaluate 16 LLMs and find that although models achieve 67 to 95% accuracy on MCQs, they fail substantially on open-ended WCD computation. For CBS, the best-performing model, GPT-5, deviates from the GT by 150.2 $μ$s on average (mean absolute error, MAE), and its predicted WCD is within 50 $μ$s of the GT for only 39.4% of flows, while all other models exceed an MAE of 250 $μ$s. Providing the WCD equations in the prompt lowers the error for 9 of 10 models on CQF but only 2 of 9 on CBS. Such errors are large relative to TSN latency budgets and can lead to violations of real-time constraints and unsafe configurations. TSNBench demonstrates that MCQ benchmarks may overestimate LLM capabilities in safety-critical networking.</p></details> |  |
| **[Can LLM Agents Be Forward-Deployed Engineers? Requirement Discovery and Delivery for Enterprise Text-to-SQL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03551v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM coding agents can increasingly automate model post-training, but whether they can act as forward-deployed engineer (FDE) agents, discovering customer requirements and turning them into deployment decisions, remains unclear. We study this on a real enterprise text-to-SQL engagement: a simulated customer, grounded in the real use case's requirements, starts with an ambiguous request. An agent must discover acceptance criteria, data permissions, and hardware constraints in the discovery phase, then plan and execute a post-training recipe in the delivery phase. Across 15 models, the strongest discover over 80% of withheld requirements and open-weight models discover 40-50%; in the delivery phase, only 25% of runs across eight coding-agent configurations pass all acceptance criteria. We identify four bottlenecks in this engagement: (1) incomplete discovery, with 40% of withheld requirements missing or misrepresented; (2) unreliable execution; (3) underused compute budgets and a lack of systematic comparisons across base models and post-training methods; and (4) benchmark accuracy does not guarantee production readiness. | **[RogueMerge: Robust and Unified Attacks against LLM Model Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03344v3)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Model merging composes specialized capabilities into a single LLM by aggregating task-specific LLMs sourced from unverified public platforms, exposing a critical supply-chain attack surface: Because any malicious behavior can be encoded into a task vector, and merging grants third-party vectors direct write access to model weights, an attacker-provided task vector can enable or amplify diverse downstream threats. (ii) Attackers have no knowledge of the victim's merging configurations, causing a static attack vector optimized in isolation to be easily diluted or neutralized. (iii) Practical threat induction must generalize to attack prompts unseen during optimization, which static vectors cannot adequately encode. In light of these gaps, we present RogueMerge, the first unified attack framework addressing all three challenges. To handle autoregressive generation, we replace static arithmetic with a joint optimization that explicitly enforces attack success after merging. To better simulate unknown merging settings, we formulate attack injection as a stochastic min-max optimization problem and solve it via meta-gradient descent. To generalize across heterogeneous attack prompts, we employ distributionally robust optimization and derive a tractable first-order Taylor approximation at LLM scale, with a provable error bound. Across four threats, six merging algorithms, and over 170 merged LLMs, RogueMerge consistently outperforms prior attacks. It also remains stable across diverse merging settings and resists standard defenses.</p></details> | <details><summary>To ap...</summary><p>To appear in IEEE Symposium on Security and Privacy (S&P), 2027</p></details> |

| **[IAMRAGBENCH: Benchmarking Authorization Expansions in LLM-Generated AWS IAM Policies under RAG Poisoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03431v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) using retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) can synthesize Amazon Web Services (AWS) Identity and Access Management (IAM) policies from tasks and operational documents. | **[IdeaTaste: Do LLMs Share the Research Taste of Human Researchers?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03424v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Research taste, the judgment of what to read, which problems to pursue, and which methods to try, increasingly shapes the work of AI scientists, yet it remains far less understood than their ability to execute research. | **[The SIMPLE Law for Energy Cost of LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03330v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generative AI is billed per token, yet tokens are not equally expensive. From these, we obtain the SIMPLE Inference Model to Predict LLMs Energy (SIMPLE), a four-parameter law that separates prefill from decode, and decode into KV-cache cost and next-token computation. | **[R$^2$-Omni: Reducing Structural and Precision Redundancy in Omni-LLMs for Long-Form Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03247v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Omni-modal large language models (Omni-LLMs) typically process large numbers of multimodal tokens from audio-visual inputs, incurring substantial computational costs and a large memory footprint for the key-value (KV) cache. We attribute this gap to query-guided selection, which can retain sufficient evidence for answer selection while discarding details needed for comprehensive generation. However, the retained representations may still contain precision redundancy due to unnecessarily high numerical precision in their cached values. | **[Cold-Start Pricing of LLM Services under Two-Sided Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03199v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Online large language model (LLM) services face a pricing cold start: providers initially lack the historical data and user information needed for precise pricing, while users' willingness to pay evolves with repeated use. Crucially, we show that standard $\mathrm{@}1$ objectives achieve these exact same optimal policies through prompt-adaptive regularization or reward transformation. An earlier version is available on Zenodo: https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23636512</p></details> |

On the tool provider side, legacy services are not directly callable through MCP; the rapid protocol development creates ongoing compatibility cost. On the agent side, the number of mounted tools is limited by the LLM context window and inference overhead; mounting a large tool set increases token usage and inference latency and can reduce task success rate. The hybrid retrieval engine sustains $\sim$98\% Top-15 recall; it scales agent tool access to 3{,}000+ tools with improved tool selection accuracy. Code available at https://github.com/Tdadada/BLPO</p></details> |

Second, researchers become more specialized, exhibiting narrower task portfolios and less overlap in their contributions with co-authors. Third, these changes are concentrated among newly formed teams, whose members have no prior collaboration ties, whereas established teams, whose members have all collaborated previously, exhibit considerably greater organizational inertia. The first era was defined by commercial smart speakers such as the original Google Home and Amazon Alexa, built on rigid, rule-based dialogue. Interactions were single-turn, user-initiated and "successful" if and only if a command matched one of a finite set of pre-templated intents. We reflect on two completed bodies of work with pre-LLM CAs and an in-progress study with a post-LLM CA. We discuss how our definition and measurement of success have evolved during this transition and also ask open questions to the community about how to optimally measure "success" in the post-LLM CA era. This research is deeply applied and subject to the messiness of real-world deployments. Our intended contributions are deliberately practical, a combination of methodological considerations and reflection questions to support researchers engaging with this new wave of technologies.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by GROUP Companion '27</p></details> |
| **[Fairway: Load-Aware KV Cache Reuse for LLM Prefill Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02082v1)** | 2026-11-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-based applications often employ agentic workflows and retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), which build long prompts from shared context. When prefixes are shared across requests, serving engines reuse the key-value (KV) cache to skip parts of the prefill computation. Reuse is typically graded: the uncached work of requests in a queue can differ by up to 100x. In this setting, the order of scheduling determines the time-to-first-token (TTFT) for the queued requests. KV reuse-first scheduling, which prioritizes the requests with the longest KV cache hit, gives the lowest median TTFT but starves expensive requests under load spikes; first-come-first-served (FCFS) scheduling bounds the tail but raises the median TTFT. We present Fairway, a prefill scheduler built on adaptive reuse prioritization: it favors KV cache reuse while queues are short, and shifts toward fair allocation as requests wait longer relative to their own prefill time. Fairway employs a virtual dual lane model: a KV reuse-first Fast lane and a Fair lane atop the serving engine's waiting queue that enables speeding up of cheap reuse requests without starving expensive ones. This model automatically adapts to the workload without any per-workload tuning. Fairway lowers the tail TTFT at no cost to the median in transient overload. Across workloads and load levels, Fairway matches or beats the best baseline, with a p99 TTFT up to 8.6x lower than prior prefix-aware schedulers. During load spikes, it keeps the tail of FCFS at a 6x lower median TTFT.</p></details> |  |
| **[HERALD: High-Throughput Block Diffusion LLM Inference via CPU-GPU Cooperative KV Cache Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21633v3)** | 2026-11-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Block diffusion LLMs (block dLLMs) improve decode throughput by generating tokens in parallel, and are emerging as a next-generation alternative to autoregressive LLMs. At long contexts, however, the KV cache remains a major throughput bottleneck in both memory bandwidth and capacity. For autoregressive LLMs, dynamic sparse attention is often combined with KV offloading to alleviate both bottlenecks, keeping the full KV cache in CPU DRAM and fetching only a critical subset to the GPU. However, KV subset selection and retrieval over bandwidth-limited PCIe incur substantial offloading overheads that often stall the GPU. We present HERALD, the first dynamic sparse attention method for block dLLMs that supports KV offloading. HERALD exploits a property of block dLLMs: a critical KV subset selected once per block is reused across all of its denoising steps, so the next block's selection can be hidden behind the current block's denoising. HERALD realizes this with a two-stage selection algorithm co-designed with its execution system. While the GPU denoises the current block, the CPU speculatively searches the full KV cache for the next block with a single query merged from the current block's B queries, cutting the selection compute by Bx; the GPU then refines the candidates into the final top-K subset with the next block's real queries. At K=1024, HERALD achieves near-lossless accuracy relative to Dense on LongBench and reasoning tasks across four block dLLMs, and improves decode throughput by up to 3.70x over Dense GPU-only inference, 2.59x over MAGE, a state-of-the-art block-dLLM sparse attention method, and 8.38x over MAGE-Offload, its direct KV-offloading extension.</p></details> |  |
| **[LARK: Lean-Assisted Reasoning for LLM-Generated GPU Kernels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02028v1)** | 2026-11-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Efficient GPU kernels are typically low-level and hardware-specific, and LLM agents can now generate them faster than experts can verify. Existing approaches rely on testing, which provides evidence but not a guarantee of correctness. We introduce LARK, an agentic framework that automatically and formally verifies GPU kernels against their PyTorch reference implementations. LARK proves equivalence in functional and memory behavior under exact arithmetic and establishes a new trust boundary: human effort is only needed to verify operator-level Lean semantics ahead of kernel generation time, instead of checking every single line of generated code. LARK formalizes the GPU machine model and the semantics of each operation in Lean 4 once per target language, and the Lean 4 semantics of any incoming kernel is derived from these pre-built definitions. LLM agents then automate the proof in two steps. First, an agent decomposes the whole kernel semantics into smaller segments and Lean 4 verifies this segmentation, which addresses the intractability of proving equivalence for large kernels directly. Second, agents write Lean proofs that each kernel segment matches its high-level PyTorch counterpart. Together, the two steps give whole-program equivalence. We demonstrate that LARK can be used 1) to ensure correctness in existing code-generation agents and 2) to automatically verify human-written kernel libraries. On the KernelCraft GPU tasks, combined with a simple ReAct agent, LARK enables GLM 5.3 to fully verify 10 of 12 Level-1 kernels and Opus 5.5 all 5 Level-2 kernels. Moreover, over 10 production kernels randomly sampled from popular open-source LLM serving frameworks, LARK verifies 9 and identifies a new data race bug in the tenth. We believe LARK represents a significant step toward verifiable code generation in the agentic era.</p></details> |  |
| **[ClickSight: An LLM Framework for Interpreting Student Clickstreams Through Learning Strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02021v1)** | 2026-11-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Digital learning environments produce rich clickstream data that can reveal how students approach learning tasks, yet interpreting these fine-grained traces often requires substantial expert effort. We introduce ClickSight, a framework for interpreting student clickstreams through learning strategies defined in the literature. Given a student clickstream and a predefined set of learning strategies, ClickSight uses a staged LLM-based pipeline to identify strategy-relevant evidence, assess adherence to each strategy, and generate an evidence-grounded interpretation of the student's behavior. We evaluate ClickSight across two learning environments, 365 students, and 12 model backbones using an expert-grounded quality rubric and an LLM judge calibrated with human-expert ratings. Our results show that ClickSight can generate high-quality strategy-based interpretations across different models and environments, with reasoning models performing more consistently than instruction-tuned counterparts. We further find that providing external clickstream-extracted guidance can improve interpretation quality for strategies that require precise quantitative information from the trace. By connecting fine-grained clickstreams with theoretically defined learning strategies and preserving the evidence underlying each judgment, ClickSight provides a flexible approach for studying student behavior in digital learning environments.</p></details> | <details><summary>29 pa...</summary><p>29 pages, 12 figures, 19 tables. Under review. Code: https://github.com/epfl-ml4ed/ClickSight-CEAI</p></details> |
| **[GRIMIP: A General Framework for Instance-Specific Configuration of MIP Solvers Using LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23299v3)** | 2026-11-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Configuring the hyperparameters of Mixed-integer programming (MIP) solvers is a high-dimensional, instance-dependent optimization problem where suboptimal settings can degrade solving time by orders of magnitude. Default configurations are often suboptimal, while traditional tuning methods either suffer from the ``cold-start'' problem and inefficient search or heavily rely on expert experience. This paper introduces \textbf{GRIMIP} (\textbf{\underline{G}}eneral \textbf{\underline{R}}easoning for \textbf{\underline{I}}nstance-specific \textbf{\underline{MIP}} configuration), a novel hybrid intelligence framework that synergistically integrates the semantic reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) with the sample-efficient search of Bayesian Optimization (BO). GRIMIP enables the LLM to function as a complete probabilistic surrogate within the BO loop, significantly improving performance and reducing sampling and evaluation costs. On seven benchmarks including MIPLIB, GRIMIP achieves over 40\% reduction in Primal-Dual Integral on hard instances, outperforming SMAC and other LLM-assisted BO methods. By granting LLMs sufficient autonomy, GRIMIP combines the expert-level reasoning of LLMs with the efficient search of BO, achieving state-of-the-art performance.</p></details> |  |
| **[RubricWorld: World Model-Guided Rubric Optimization for LLM Graders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01986v1)** | 2026-11-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Rubric optimization improves LLM grading by revising scoring instructions while keeping the grader's parameters fixed. Existing optimizers often judge edits by aggregate validation performance, limiting the supervision they retain about which errors an edit corrects or introduces. Testing each candidate also requires rescoring validation answers, which limits how many edits can be explored within a fixed budget. We propose RubricWorld, a framework that learns from previous edit evaluations to guide further search. Its world model predicts an edit's correction and degradation rates at score boundaries, conditioned on the current rubric state. The training signal comes from paired grading results: scores for the same answers before and after an edit. Predictions select one candidate for direct evaluation, and held-out confidence bounds determine whether it corrects enough target errors while keeping new errors within prespecified limits. Measured effects update the model even when the edit is rejected. Across EIR (National), ASAP2.0, and ASAP-SAS with three frozen LLM graders, RubricWorld achieves the highest score in 20 of 27 metric comparisons. On ASAP2.0, it raises gpt-5-mini accuracy from 18.20% with the original rubric to 46.70%, exceeding the strongest baseline, APO, by 22.60 percentage points. code will be released upon acceptance.</p></details> | <details><summary>25 pa...</summary><p>25 pages including references and appendices; 6 figures, 8 tables</p></details> |
| **[Beyond Prompt Injection: The Tool Update Gap in LLM Agent Security](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01976v1)** | 2026-11-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model based agents increasingly rely on external tools to invoke services and interact with web resources. These tools can evolve through software updates, yet agentic frameworks may continue trusting them without re-verifying their safety after implementation changes. This tool update gap creates a supply-chain vulnerability in agentic systems: because updated tool code executes outside the LLM's context window, malicious functionality embedded in tool updates can bypass defenses centered on model-visible interactions, leaving agents exposed to stealthy, persistent threats that existing in-context defenses can hardly address. To thoroughly investigate this threat, we introduce ETUDE (Exploiting Tool Updates for Direct Execution), an attack that embeds malicious functionality into updates of initially benign tools to produce compromised tools, while preserving their legitimate tool functionality. Unlike prompt-based attacks, ETUDE operates entirely outside the LLM's context and requires no adversarial instructions, thereby bypassing defenses restricted to model-visible content. We evaluate ETUDE across three representative attack workloads and seven widely used commercial and open-source agentic frameworks, including Gemini CLI, Codex CLI, and Claude Code. The results show that ETUDE achieves a 97.6% ASR while maintaining a 95.9% legitimate task completion rate. We further examine existing defenses and find that they provide insufficient protection against this threat. These findings expose a fundamental blind spot in current agent security practices: trust established for a tool does not necessarily remain valid after its implementation updates.</p></details> | 12 pages, 5 figures |
| **[Towards Self-Evolving Narrative Systems: Real-Time Adaptation and Long-Term Optimization with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01954v1)** | 2026-11-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Traditional interactive narrative systems offer strong authorial control and structural coherence but often restrict player agency. In contrast, LLM-driven narratives enable open-ended generation and greater flexibility, yet frequently suffer from inconsistency and diminished narrative control. To navigate this trade-off, we propose a hybrid narrative framework that dynamically balances pre-authored content with real-time LLM generation via semantic similarity alignment. Additionally, sustaining narrative quality over extended gameplay remains a core challenge in LLM-based storytelling. To address this, we introduce a self-evolving, feedback-driven optimization framework that leverages in-context learning to iteratively refine prompts, eliminating the need for manual prompt engineering. Through a case study and participant-based evaluation, we show that our framework enhances narrative coherence while preserving player agency, effectively aligning generated content with both authorial intent and evolving player behavior.</p></details> | <details><summary>Under...</summary><p>Under review at the ACL Rolling Review (ARR) October 2026 cycle</p></details> |
| **[No Note Is an Island: Probing LLMs' Understanding of Crowdsourced Fact-Checking Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01935v1)** | 2026-11-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) increasingly assist crowdsourced fact-checking, yet evaluations focus on individual annotations. A correction's reach and reception depend on visibility, audience responses, and platform policies. We introduce correction context understanding: assessing corrective content, interpreting audience responses, and reasoning about platform dynamics within a shared environment. We build NoteHabitat from 36 months of Weibo Annotations data, linking 102,994 posts (42,853 annotations), 431,407 comments, and five policy events. Evaluating diverse model families across seven tasks spanning content, interaction, and management reveals three gaps. Annotation quality correlates only weakly with engagement ($ρ=0.068$), while four zero-shot LLMs systematically underrate quality relative to human judgments, especially evidence support and objectivity. Models also miss toxic comments supporting corrections (36.4% average recall across ten models). At the platform level, LLMs partially predict policy-associated outcome directions, but LLM-guided simulations poorly reproduce daily activity. Cross-group analyses expose blind spots tied to contribution types and reader stances that aggregate scores conceal. Reliable fact-checking AI must understand the note, the conversation around it, and the platform in which both unfold.</p></details> |  |
| **[RECEIPT: Measuring and Repairing the Return Assumption of LLM Agents in Web Service Operations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01930v1)** | 2026-11-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents operate Web infrastructure from the command line and rely on a return assumption, namely that each tool call returns within a bounded time and with its requested action finished. Many commands, however, ask a question before they act, and the agent's harness either closes the program's input or keeps it open as a terminal at which nobody types. With closed input, the program can return status 0 without the requested action. With open input, it waits until a time limit, and the agent pays in time and tokens. To the best of our knowledge, this paper is the first to measure and repair the return assumption. We collect 91,469 production tool calls and 89,047 public agent trajectories, test how 8 widely used harnesses launch commands, and build Litmus, a catalog of 158 checked command-line invocations, and Tripwire, a benchmark of 25 task pairs that differ only in whether a question guards the requested action. We then propose RECEIPT, which keeps the input open, reads what each process waits for from system calls, and returns this state to an unmodified agent through a model-checked protocol. On Litmus, closed input causes 11.5% of tested invocations to return status 0 without performing the requested action, and 7 of 8 harnesses close input when no terminal is attached. An unanswered question at a plain terminal multiplies a task's token use by 2.9, and the input connection moves an LLM from 7th to 3rd place among 8. Across 5 LLMs, RECEIPT reduces false claims of success by 73.5% and completes all 25 guarded tasks on a second harness. An agent's reliability therefore depends on what its harness lets it observe as well as on its LLM. Agent builders should report what each tool call did, not only how it ended, and benchmark maintainers should fix and report the input connection before they rank LLMs.</p></details> |  |
| **[Does reflective LLM feedback survive a wet-lab-scale label budget?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01908v1)** | 2026-11-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Can reflective language-model feedback improve a biological predictor when each cycle buys only one plate of labels? We separate three possible sources of benefit: proposing model edits, choosing measurements, and revising decisions through repeated feedback. We replay $36$ ProteinGym assays, starting with $96$ labels and acquiring $96$ per round for six rounds, with a sealed test set and a shared edit schema. The pre-specified comparison of full LLM control against random edits detects no advantage in test rank correlation ($Δz=-0.008$, $95\%$ CI $[-0.034,+0.018]$); equivalence is also unestablished. Exploratory ablations nevertheless identify useful model editing. With random acquisition fixed, LLM edits improve Fisher-transformed test Spearman by $0.043$ over no edits; with acquisition fixed to upper confidence bound, they improve normalized top-variant recovery by $0.023$. LLM acquisition alone does not detectably outperform fixed adaptive rules, and repeated feedback does not detectably outperform one initial recommendation under full control. Thus, the observed gains support constrained specialist editing, while the incremental value of recursive reflection remains unestablished at this label budget.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the NeurIPS 2026 Agentic AI for Biological Discovery Workshop. OpenReview: https://openreview.net/forum?id=ezGV5BIXVJ</p></details> |
| **[Don't Guess, Ask Wisely: Benchmarking and Training Proactive Elicitation in Open-Domain LLM Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01878v1)** | 2026-11-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When user queries are incomplete, large language models should ask targeted questions before committing to an answer, a capability we call proactive elicitation, yet evaluation on real user-assistant queries across seven LLMs (8B to frontier) shows that fewer than 11% of underspecified queries trigger a follow-up question. To benchmark and train this capability, we introduce VagueBench, where each seed query is annotated with structured intent and detail units then degraded into intent-vague and fully-vague variants via controlled removal of linguistic cues, yielding known hidden information sets for evaluating both when and what to ask. We propose DialogGRPO, an offline reinforcement learning method that organizes multi-turn elicitation as a dialogue-state directed acyclic graph, where candidate questions at each node receive dense turn-level rewards combining immediate information gain with lookahead value through group-relative comparison, enabling fine-grained credit assignment without runtime simulation. DialogGRPO elicits missing information on over 88% of vague queries with substantial gains in both recovery efficiency and answer coverage, without over-asking on complete queries, outperforms prompting and test-time-scaling baselines, and generalizes zero-shot to MultiWOZ 2.4 and AmbigQA.</p></details> | <details><summary>26 pa...</summary><p>26 pages, 6 figures; EMNLP 2026 oral</p></details> |
| **[NEIGHBOR: Aligning Service Behaviour and Evidence Use in an LLM Agent for Community Resident Services](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01836v1)** | 2026-11-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Residents who contact a community service desk about a repair or a service charge need answers they can act on, with facts that hold for their case and commitments the desk can keep. In tool-augmented LLM agents, such facts come from documents and records, and the conduct of a service answer is usually specified in a system prompt. We present NEIGHBOR, an LLM agent for community resident services that trains this conduct into the model that drives its tools. NEIGHBOR is deployed on a live resident-service platform for a district of an East Asian city of more than ten million people. It adapts Qwen3-235B-A22B with LoRA on 7,824 supervised fine-tuning examples built from public community-service materials around six service-behaviour targets, among them commitment control and human referral, and the adapted model plans document retrieval and read-only text-to-SQL over the resident's own records. In an offline evaluation with seven configurations, an independent domain expert rated all 350 answers to 50 stratified held-out queries. NEIGHBOR scores 7.74/10 against 5.86 for the same agent running the un-tuned model (+1.88; paired Wilcoxon $p<10^{-8}$; higher on 46 of 50 queries) and improves all six rated dimensions. No tool-augmented answer received a red-line flag (standalone models: 8-14% of answers). An LLM judge ranks the seven configurations much as the expert does (Spearman $ρ=0.93$). Its rubric counts specifics absent from the reference answer as fabricated, so it scores tool-grounded answers 1-2 points lower, enough to change which configuration ranks first. For resident-facing agents, service behaviour is a practical fine-tuning target that raises expert-rated answer quality with the tools held fixed.</p></details> | <details><summary>9 pag...</summary><p>9 pages, 6 figures, 5 tables</p></details> |
| **[When Should LLMs Be Less Specific? Selective Abstraction for Reliable Long-Form Text Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.11908v4)** | 2026-11-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLMs are widely used, yet they remain prone to factual errors that erode trust and limit adoption in high-risk settings. One approach to mitigate this risk is to equip models with uncertainty estimation mechanisms that abstain when confidence is low. However, this binary "all-or-nothing" approach is excessively restrictive in long-form settings, often discarding valuable information. We introduce Selective Abstraction (SA), a framework that enables LLMs to trade specificity for reliability by selectively reducing the detail of uncertain content. We formalize SA through selective risk and coverage, and propose Atom-wise Selective Abstraction, a claim-level instantiation that decomposes responses into atomic claims and replaces uncertain atoms with higher-confidence, less specific abstractions. We develop a novel end-to-end pipeline for evaluating long-form generation that instantiates risk as factual correctness and measures coverage as retained information. Our comprehensive empirical evaluation across five LLMs and two long-form factual benchmarks shows that atom-wise SA consistently improves the risk-coverage trade-off, outperforming seven baselines, including claim removal, self-revision, sampling-based aggregation, and chain-of-verification. It improves AURC by up to 35.61% over claim removal, demonstrating that reducing specificity boosts reliability while preserving substantial information. We further provide a risk-guided procedure for selecting a confidence threshold that satisfies a target risk.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted as poster to NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[SkillDAG: Self-Evolving Typed Skill Graphs for LLM Skill Selection at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03056v4)** | 2026-11-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large skill libraries are now standard equipment for LLM agents, and which subset reaches the prompt decides what the agent can do. Enumeration and top-$K$ embedding retrieval both treat skills as independent documents, assuming relevance is a property of one skill at a time. It is not: skills depend on, conflict with, specialize, and duplicate one another, so the closest match to a task can name it while omitting a prerequisite it needs. Graph retrieval encodes these relations but spends them in a fixed pipeline, handing the agent an opaque bundle. We present SkillDAG, which exposes a typed skill graph to the LLM as an agent-callable interface rather than consuming it for the agent: search returns vector matches, typed-edge neighbors, and conflict signals, and a propose-then-commit protocol lets it register execution-backed edges as it works. Across two benchmarks and three model families, SkillDAG outperforms full-library prompting, vector retrieval, and Graph-of-Skills, gaining +14.3 points of ALFWorld success and tying only at ceiling; its ranking holds flat across a $10\times$ pool expansion that degrades the fixed pipeline. Inter-skill structure, these results suggest, is worth more to an agent that can query and revise it than to a retriever that consumes it.</p></details> | <details><summary>In *T...</summary><p>In *The 2026 Conference on Empirical Methods in Natural Language Processing (EMNLP, Findings)*, 2026</p></details> |
| **[OmniCast: Interaction-Aware Serving for Real-Time Omni-Modal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22983v2)** | 2026-11-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Realtime omni-modal LMs support speech-centric conversations where users stream inputs, hear generated audio, and interrupt freely. Existing Omni-LM serving systems still rely on throughput-oriented LLM scheduling and LRU KV offloading. These policies ignore audio playback and multi-turn reuse: they may generate tokens far beyond what users hear, wasting work after barge-in, and evict KV state needed in the next turn. OmniCast is an interaction-aware serving system for realtime Omni-LM interaction. It exposes playback progress, speech activity, and barge-in events to the serving pipeline. The scheduler prioritizes first-audio and near-underrun sessions while limiting generation beyond the playback frontier. The KV manager uses next-use-aware eviction and preloads likely-needed KV during user speech to hide reload latency. On vLLM-Omni, OmniCast improves realtime serving across two Omni-LMs and mixed workloads. It lowers P90 audio TTFP by $1.55\times$ on average and up to $2.21\times$, while improving completed-request throughput by $1.15\times$ on average and up to $1.56\times$, and moves most KV reload work off the next-turn critical path.</p></details> |  |
